| **[Local AI Agents in Power Electronics Simulation: Deployment, Measurements and Costs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24880v1)** | 2026-11-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agentic artificial intelligence (AI) is emerging in power electronics, where it drives the simulation loop that engineers used to close largely by hand. In this loop, a converter design, or a study of the network it connects to, is built, run, measured and revised until it satisfies its specification. Such an agent can be served from a cloud API or from an inference device on the premises. For organizations whose designs, customer data or export-controlled material cannot leave the site, and whose simulators are licensed there, local deployment is the natural choice. In such a deployment, the agent becomes an electrical load on the organization's own equipment, i.e., every run consumes power and energy on a local device, generates tokens and takes time. However, existing work focuses mainly on what the agent can achieve with cloud-hosted models. What a local deployment costs in power, energy, tokens and time on real simulation tasks, and what its choices trade against one another, has received little quantitative investigation under comparable conditions. To address this gap, a systematic evaluation framework is presented in this paper. It applies locally served models to agentic power electronics tasks, from the converter level to the system level, and measures their power, energy, token and time costs. Based on these measurements, the capability boundary of a local agent on such tasks and the cost per solved task are characterized. The results are expected to provide practical insight for the use of agentic AI in power electronics and the power systems it serves.</p></details> | <details><summary>27 pa...</summary><p>27 pages, 23 figures, 12 tables</p></details> |

| **[Vibe-Trading: Towards Agent-Native Trading](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24845v1)** | 2026-11-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Existing AI agents can retrieve information, call tools, run experiments, and produce multi-step outputs. | **[Kaer: Security-Aware Replanning for Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24839v1)** | 2026-11-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>One popular architecture for multi-agent systems (MAS) relies on an orchestrator that generates an initial plan, coordinates its execution, and dynamically revises the plan based on agent feedback when tasks cannot be fully specified in advance. | **[LoongSage: A Production-Grade, High-Efficiency Agentic RL Framework for Frontier LLMs with Validated Recipes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16352v3)** | 2026-11-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reinforcement learning (RL) has emerged as a critical phase in the post-training pipeline of large language models (LLMs). We evaluate four representative coding agents, and further evaluate whitelist-based execution control and LLM review. Our analysis uncovers recurring trade-offs along three foundational axes: autonomy levels, agentic roles, and the VA workflow. We address these with parallel training runs, an objective calibrated to match online impact, and careful context engineering. In one campaign, the system ran 1,097 completed candidate experiments and accepted 325, a 29.6\% acceptance rate compared with 15.6\% in the earlier campaign under a different objective and search constraint. Yet passing tests alone does not reveal accumulating complexity or gaps in test coverage, which then persist as agents extend their own code. We introduce metrics-in-the-loop, an inference-time intervention that gives agents in-flight feedback: cheap, automatically computed code metrics injected into the agent's context during the trajectory. After each code-changing agent turn, we insert short feedback messages naming changed functions whose cyclomatic complexity exceeds a limit (complexity feedback) or changed lines that are skipped by tests (coverage feedback), giving the agent the opportunity to refactor code or strengthen tests before continuing. We run extensive experiments and ablations comparing several interventions across three long-horizon coding benchmarks and four strong models. We find that complexity feedback significantly reduces erosion, a measure of complexity concentration of code, without affecting held-out correctness of the code. Broadly, our experiments investigate a practical form of agent "re-railing", where timely, actionable measurements redirect agents before weaknesses become the foundation they keep building on. As agents scale to codebases with millions of lines, such interventions become critical to sustaining effective development and long-term code maintainability.</p></details> | <details><summary>Agent...</summary><p>Agent Evaluation Science Symposium, Fall 2026, New York City</p></details> |
| **[Continual Tool-Space Extension Learning for Multimodal Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23810v1)** | 2026-11-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal agents largely rely on generic tools such as cropping, search, and code execution, leaving specialist vision models outside their tool space. However, bringing such a model into an already trained agent is not straightforward. The challenge is to learn each new tool efficiently while retaining the ability to use existing ones. This work proposes COTEX, continual tool-space extension learning, that adds new tools at efficient adapter-level cost. COTEX locates two sets of parameter directions; one triggers a tool call and thereby opens a multi-turn reasoning trajectory, while the other determines which tool is chosen. The adapter of a new tool is restricted to the calling directions that leave earlier tool choices unaffected by orthogonal constraint. We further introduce a protocol that converts specialist models' existing task-specific annotations into verified tool-use trajectories. Experiments on two representative agents and three specialist tools show gains on ten vision-language benchmarks, with at most a 0.3-point drop on existing tools.</p></details> |  |
| **[AgentAbstain: Do LLM Agents Know When Not to Act?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10059v2)** | 2026-11-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agent systems based on large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed for autonomous tasks, yet existing evaluations mostly focus on task success rather than whether agents know when to abstain. This gap poses real risks: under ambiguity, conflicting constraints, or tool failures, agents may execute unintended and irreversible actions. To close this gap, we present the first systematic evaluation framework for agentic abstention: the calibrated ability of tool-using LLM agents to recognize when not to act. At its core, AgentAbstain is a paired-task benchmark built on an agent-native taxonomy of 8 abstention scenarios across pre-execution reasoning and runtime discovery. It contains 263 paired tasks across 42 executable sandbox environments, where each pair consists of a should-act task and a should-abstain variant produced through a controlled perturbation to the instruction, tool, or environment state. To scale this paired design and resist data contamination, we propose AbstainGen, a fully automated pipeline that synthesizes sandbox environments and generates paired tasks end-to-end, validated by deterministic replay and semantic LLM judges; fresh task instances can be regenerated on demand, and three independent annotators rate 94-98% of sampled tasks as well-designed. Across 17 frontier LLMs in 4 agent harnesses, the best agent (Gemini 3.1 Pro) achieves only 59.5% paired accuracy (correct on both the act and abstain sides of each paired task). More importantly, abstention capability is largely independent of general task-solving capability, indicating that scaling task-solving alone will not close this gap. We further identify failure modes such as post-hoc abstention, in which agents execute irreversible actions before recognizing abstention triggers. Our code and dataset are open-sourced at agentabstain.github.io.</p></details> | 65 pages, 13 figures |
| **[Thinking Fast, Anywhere: One System-1 for the Decision Points of Any Agent Workflow](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23751v1)** | 2026-11-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Autonomous LLM agents incur substantial cost and latency by invoking a large model (System-2) at every step, even when many intermediate actions choose among a small set of known candidates. Existing dual-system agents offload such decisions to a lightweight System-1 model, but rely on workflow-specific hooks authored per environment. We present AnyS1, a framework that turns human-authored procedural skills, whose instructions naturally specify where choices arise and which options apply, into a shared, domain-general source of decision points. Offline, AnyS1 mines grounded enumerable decisions across over 1,000 public skills, gathers execution-grounded trajectory states inside isolated containers to construct Any-DP, and trains a single calibrated 4B pointer System-1 with label smoothing. At run time, compiled workflows resolve routine decisions in one forward pass and fall back to System-2 when confidence falls below a calibrated threshold. Across three unseen benchmarks and distinct System-2 model families, AnyS1 preserves task reliability while cutting episode cost by 75-91% and model-call time by 63-92% relative to an uncompiled System-2 agent, and maintains performance under zero-shot threshold transfer.</p></details> | <details><summary>16 pa...</summary><p>16 pages, 8 figures, 7 tables. Project page: https://middle-chunjie.github.io/AnyS1/</p></details> |
| **[A Comparison of Heuristic, Human-Sensitive, and Self-Play DRL Policies in Human-Agent Teaming](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23747v1)** | 2026-11-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Dynamical Perceptual-Motor Primitives (DPMPs) offer a tractable framework for generating human-like movement dynamics in artificial agents (AAs) and have been used to support coordination in human-AI teaming tasks. Traditionally, DPMP controllers rely on heuristic (rule-based) decision policies derived from human behavior to guide goal selection and action sequencing. Although effective in structured tasks, such heuristics can be difficult to generalize to more complex settings, motivating interest in deep reinforcement learning (DRL) approaches for learning decision policies for human-AI interaction. A key challenge, however, is ensuring that DRL policies remain aligned with human strategies, particularly when agents are trained through self-play. To examine this issue, we compared three target-selection policies governing DPMP-based AAs in a first-person herding task: (1) a human-derived heuristic policy, (2) a self-play DRL policy (SP-DRL), and (3) a human-sensitive DRL policy (HS-DRL) trained alongside a heuristic agent. Results showed that heuristic agents most closely matched typical human-human coordination patterns, followed by HS-DRL agents, whereas SP-DRL agents deviated most. These findings highlight the importance of human-informed training when developing DRL-based teammates and suggest that simple heuristic policies may remain an effective approach for designing human-aligned AAs capable of human-AI collaboration.</p></details> | <details><summary>9 pag...</summary><p>9 pages, 5 figures. Includes Supplementary Material A (1 page, 0 figures), B (1 page, 0 figures), and C (54 pages, 216 figures). Submitted to IEEE HMS for possible publication</p></details> |
| **[From Tasks to Turns: Measuring Human Labor Contributions in Human-Agent Joint Production](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26365v2)** | 2026-11-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In agent workflows, a brief human instruction can alter subsequent search, reasoning, and tool execution, yet final task outcomes rarely reveal the contribution of an individual instruction to human-agent joint production. We propose a Human Turn-level framework for measuring human contributions, using task quality and token usage to characterize the collaborative production outcomes associated with each human instruction. We compare continuations from adjacent checkpoints under matched models, budgets, and termination conditions, and assess stability through repeated measurements. Across four archived and simulated interaction settings, we conduct 29,124 comparisons between predicted and observed directions of quality and computational change at both Human Turn and session levels. On HumanBench, we obtain 506 valid comparisons across 85 real-user scenarios. Results show that quality and computational changes associated with human guidance depend on task conditions. Expert-style and ordinary-style guidance differ in information provision, correction, and repetition. Even when task quality after continuation is identical, remaining computational demands can differ substantially across checkpoints. Our framework advances the measurement of human labor contributions from tasks and sessions to individual turns, providing a repeatable, fine-grained foundation for studying human contributions and their allocation in human-agent joint production.</p></details> | <details><summary>18 pa...</summary><p>18 pages, 6 figures, 19 tables, including appendices</p></details> |
| **[GTA: Generative Tower of Agents for Scalable Holonic Scene Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23727v1)** | 2026-11-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Scalable world modeling requires coordinating scene-wide goals with fine-grained objects and dynamic characters under limited time and context budgets. We introduce GTA (Generative Tower of Agents), which represents every unit at every level of an adaptive scene hierarchy as a holon, both a self-contained construction task and a composable scene unit. Parallel agents recursively plan the hierarchy top-down, generate assets, and assemble and refine them bottom-up. Uniform interfaces carry scene-wide requirements down the hierarchy, guiding local construction so that each holon fits into the larger one. GTA accelerates scalable world modeling through high-throughput parallel execution while preserving well-structured layouts and fine-grained asset details. GTA then populates the assembled environments with dynamic human characters whose appearance and motion are guided by the scene, yielding realized 4D worlds. Project page: https://zt-zeng-tao.github.io/GTA/</p></details> | <details><summary>https...</summary><p>https://zt-zeng-tao.github.io/GTA/</p></details> |
| **[ProteinGeoBench: Frontier Structural-Biology Agents Are Fluent at Lookup but Lost in Geometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26829v2)** | 2026-11-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>ProteinGeoBench is a set of 131 tasks that give an agent protein structure files and ask it to look up recorded facts, check sequences, calculate geometry from atom coordinates or reason about structures. We ran four frontier models on every task several times (4,249 runs) and 26 models on a subset. A single overall score hides two different skills. Three of the four frontier models are best at looking up facts and worst at calculating from coordinates: Claude Opus 5 scores 0.907 on lookup and 0.716 on calculation. Gemini 3.1 Pro is the exception. Geometry is overrepresented among the hardest tasks. Half of the twelve tasks that all four models fail are calculations, although calculations are a fifth of the suite, and eight of the twelve were solved by none of the 26 models. The three leading models are within 0.031 of one another, too close to order, and with one run per task a different model ranks first in 24% of bootstrap resamples. Reports should therefore give scores by task type and the number of runs per task. They should also state the agent harness, the software that runs the model, and its network access, because both change scores. On a 15-task subset, blocking eight structural databases lowered scores, and removing network access lowered them more. Replacing our harness with each provider's own command-line agent moved scores by up to 0.09 in either direction. Across the 26 models, those that scored higher on the other tasks also scored higher on calculation.</p></details> | <details><summary>24 pa...</summary><p>24 pages, 2 figures, 10 tables. Benchmark and tasks: https://github.com/Protege-AI/ProteinGeoBench</p></details> |
| **[CommitKV: Commit-Aligned KV Cache Compression for Multimodal Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07855v2)** | 2026-11-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal agents gather visual evidence through reasoning and tool use, extending their ability to solve tasks beyond the initial input. However, accumulating observations increases KV cache memory and inference costs. Token eviction reduces these costs by retaining useful cached tokens. Existing methods use attention, redundancy, or stage-specific queries to rank tokens, without explicitly comparing the same history across tool interactions. When observations shift attention, they may mistake temporarily reduced influence for lost usefulness and evict information needed later. We propose CommitKV, a training-free KV cache compression method that accounts for this changing evidence use. Commit-aligned measurement compares the deletion effects of the same page before a tool call completes and after its observation returns. Lifecycle-aware compression retires pages with low influence in both windows and temporarily protects pages influential before the call. Joint scoring combines attention importance, key redundancy, and token-level deletion effects to fill the remaining budget. Extensive experiments cover four open-weight multimodal models and eight benchmarks. CommitKV achieves the highest average accuracy among the compared compression methods on all four models. On Qwen3-VL-8B, it achieves 69.85% at a 512-token budget, only 0.91% below FullKV, and exceeds the strongest compression baseline by 8.44% at 256 tokens. Deploying the Qwen3-VL-8B agent with SGLang and vLLM at a 256-token budget reduces peak allocated GPU memory by 54.37%-55.23% and accelerates end-to-end inference by 2.12-2.17$\times$ over FullKV on the matched workload.</p></details> |  |
| **[Auditable Clinical Support with Decision Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23700v1)** | 2026-11-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Clinicians reviewing a diagnostic work-up will ask what was considered at each step and why the assessment changed. Agents powered by free text can reach physician-level diagnostic accuracy, but their written reasoning does not always reflect the factors that went into the decision. Here, we present Decision2Care, a decision agent that works up the acutely ill patient without producing any text. A decision model assigns a probability to each permitted EHR action, and fixed rules carry out the most probable one. We evaluated Decision2Care on the records of 546 emergency admissions across 12 diseases. Decision2Care reached a diagnostic accuracy of 86.5%, compared with 86.1% for the MIRA-v2 agent, and required a median of 2.2 s per work-up, compared with 92-100 s. Each work-up tracked which options Decision2Care was considering at each step. In 10 of the 12 diseases, the differential diagnosis changed most at the test on which the guideline bases the diagnosis. Where Decision2Care was most confident after a single work-up, all but four of its diagnoses were correct (98.5%). We traced each of these four errors back to a single imaging report. Three of them were corrected when that report was removed. The same kind of decision was used to select the treatments, and Decision2Care's drug orders were judged guideline-adherent more often than MIRA-v2's. In emergency patients, its prediction of death was comparable to that of three intensivists on the same cases. In five of the eight intensive care cohorts, it exceeded the best of the three 24-h severity scores within 2-6 h of records. Our results show that decision agents can carry out clinical work-ups while recording the options, probabilities, and execution rules behind each action for audit.</p></details> | <details><summary>74 pa...</summary><p>74 pages, 6 main figures, 19 supplementary figures</p></details> |
| **[AStockArena: Beyond Returns, Can We Entrust Real Capital to the Decisions of Financial Agents?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00515v2)** | 2026-11-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used in dynamic financial trading, yet real-time trading benchmarks mainly evaluate agents by cumulative returns and risk-adjusted performance. Such outcome metrics cannot show whether trades are faithfully reported, supported by available evidence, and consistent with account states. We introduce AStockArena, a real-time decision-auditing benchmark for continuous trading in China's A-share market. Under shared market conditions and execution constraints, we evaluate 13 open- and closed-source models over 3,198 sequential decisions. AStockArena reconstructsthe actions actually posted to accounts by aligning reports, trade requests, execution responses, and account states, and audits them with eight checks spanning execution traceability (O), evidence sufficiency (RK), and financial meta-awareness (B), combining record verification with consensus expert annotation. Of 1,070 trading points, 72.99% exhibit at least one audit gap; model-level rates range from52.2% to 91.2%, with evidence gaps most prevalent. Historical trade-filtering replay further shows thatportfolio outcomes are sensitive to these flagged trades. Decision-process auditing thus offers a verifiable complement to return-based evaluation of financial agents.</p></details> |  |
| **[The Value Semantics Layer: Enabling Machine-Understandable Databases for Agentic AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23629v1)** | 2026-11-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As data systems increasingly support autonomous LLM-powered agents alongside human analysts, they require richer semantic infrastructure bridging analytical concepts and analytical operations. Existing metadata systems expose schemas, catalogs, lineage, and governance information, but provide limited support for representing operational semantics. We propose the Value Semantics Layer (VSL), an architectural abstraction that externalizes value semantics as persistent, planner-visible infrastructure. VSL introduces Value Semantic Types (VSTs), reusable operational semantic assertions capturing distributional, relational, process, quantitative, and policy-grounded semantics. Unlike descriptive metadata, VSTs enable planners to validate analytical operations, select appropriate operators, and guide analytical workflows. We present the motivation, formalization, architecture, and prototype realizations of VSL, and outline a research agenda toward AI-native semantic infrastructure for analytical systems.</p></details> |  |
| **[Agent Verifiers in Counterfactual Analysis: A Pilot Study Benchmarking Agentic Heterogeneous Treatment Effects](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23621v1)** | 2026-11-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agents can expand the covariate space of randomized experiments by retrieving fast-moving local context missed by slow-changing survey baseline covariates, helping characterize who benefits most from an intervention. Verifying such covariates is difficult: individual treatment effects are unobserved, retrieval leakage can pull post-treatment information into nominally pretreatment summaries, and encoder leakage can inject post-treatment pretraining knowledge into representations even after temporal filtering of inputs. Guided by a two-channel information bound separating summary provenance from encoder weights, we propose a three-layer benchmark protocol: (i) retrieval admissibility via pre-treatment date filtering; (ii) heterogeneity validation scored rank-weighted average treatment effects (RATEs); and (iii) encoder admissibility checked with chronologically vintaged encoders, future-fact probes, and weight perturbations. Across 11 datasets anchored by Uganda's Youth Opportunities Program, we compare session-isolated retrieval (sandboxed, bounded-budget agent runs per study) against a persistent workspace teammate (xAI's GrokBot) that builds one date-verified corpus per study while blinded to outcomes. The workspace teammate yields higher mean RATE ratios in 6/11 datasets for text alone and 7 for combined features, though paired tests show neither workflow universally dominates. Even on fixed retrieved text, encoder choice significantly shifts heterogeneity signal. Chronologically vintaged probes show no clear evidence of weight leakage, but single-record adapter perturbation analyses show that memorized future facts, once present, persist until injected noise degrades model output. Temporal filtering of retrieval is necessary but insufficient: verifying agent-generated covariates requires jointly examining source provenance, out-of-sample treatment-effect signal, and encoder weights.</p></details> | <details><summary>NeurI...</summary><p>NeurIPS 2026 Workshop: Who Verifies the Agents? Toward Reliable Agent Development</p></details> |
| **[MANGO Arena: Same Seeds, Different Chains Evaluating LLM Agents in a Disrupted Supply Chain](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23613v1)** | 2026-11-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We introduce MANGO Arena, an open-source testbed in which four LLM agents each run one company in a serial supply chain adapted from MANGO, a classroom factory-management game. The environment enforces hard rules (a truck limit, a no-credit cash constraint and production capacity), logs every blocked attempt to break them, and gives agents a public message board on which side payments are explicitly forbidden. This lets one environment measure three things at once: an economic outcome (the bullwhip effect), rule adherence under pressure, and proposals to collude. In a pilot with three small reasoning models, two scripted reference policies, two conditions (with and without a supplier fire) and three demand seeds, all LLM agents amplified demand variability as it moved upstream, by 7 to 25 times at the Retailer and 13 to 179 times at the Supplier, often more than a textbook order-up-to policy. They did so largely by ordering in batches (zero orders in up to 60% of decisions). Real rule violations were rare (at most 7 of 288 decisions per model) and no agent proposed a money side deal. The pilot's main lesson concerns evaluation method. API failures (rate limits and runaway hidden reasoning) entered the metrics silently as "no order" decisions. And when we reran games with the same seeds and no failed calls, upstream bullwhip changed by up to 17 times between runs. With three seeds, conclusions about how a disruption affects a given model did not survive a rerun. We report these failures in full and propose reporting practices for multi-agent evaluations of this kind. Code, prompts and every logged decision are public.</p></details> | 8 pages, 2 figures, |
